2.   RECOGNITION  
A.   TSD Mission Moment— Smith Middle School “U-Knighted Hour” 
This past summer, a group of interested teachers from Smith Middle School 
met to develop a “homeroom” for all Smith students.  As a building, Smith 
recognized the need to develop a stronger bond between staff and students 
and students between grade levels.  A plan was made to implement a 7 -
period day once a month and have “U-Knighted” time during the 7 th hour.  
Students were arranged by grade level and then divided amongst 41 
teachers resulting in  approximately 17 students in each group.  The 
committee meets before each U -Knighted Hour and develops a lesson plan 
for the groups.  The focus has been on character deve lopment, diversity, 
motivation, community, goal setting, and FUN!!!   Th is program has been 
well-received by both students and staff alike.   
Recommended Action:  Invite Amy Buchanan, Assistant Principal, Smith 
Middle School, Counselor Jen Hartenburg, and social studies teacher Glen Rexer,  
up to share a bit more about this program.

B.  Oakland County Outstanding Teacher of the Year Awards 
The Troy School District has many outstanding teachers on its staff. This 
evening we are proud to recognize the accomplishments of 38 individuals 
who were nominated for  the Troy School District Outstanding Teacher 
Award Program.  
The nominees represent exemplary educators at the elementary, middle 
and high school levels. They were nominated by their peers and by

students. These teachers have had a tremendous impact on the lives of 
so many through their dedication and commitment to their students. Their 
nomination letters each described how the professionals we honor here 
tonight go above and beyond to ensure that their students learn, grow

RECOGNITION  
Mission Moment 
Troy College & Career Dual 
who are enrolled in college classes while completing their high 
school course requirements.  Mrs. Deb Linford-MacDonald, 
principal of Troy College & Career High School, was invited to 
the podium to share about the Dual Enrollment program and 
explained that the program has greatly expanded from eight 
students who, over nine years ago, started classes at Baker 
Community College while attending Niles Community H igh 
School.  Currently, 44 students (including nine from Intersect) 
are enrolled in between one and four classes at Oakland and 
Macomb Community Colleges and even Oakland Universi ty 
while completing high school course requirements.  Nearly 
thirty percent of Troy College & Career students (both in and 
outside of the Troy School District) are currently enrolled in 
college.  The six students who are being recognized this 
evening exemplify the spectrum of learners we serve -  varying 
from one who is finishing her second year at OCC to  our 
newest student who is taking his first college course.  They 
represent many who are choosing to attend TCC specifically for 
its dual enrollment opportunities. These students a ll 
completed a College Prep course to get them ready for post-
secondary education and now are enrolled in our College 
Seminar class in which they receive personal assistance with 
time management and class organization, content areas, essay 
writing, study and test-taking, educational career planning, 
learning how to apply for scholarships, monitoring of grades, 
9
Enrollment Students 
The Board honored a special group of high school students 
and much more.  Additionally, collaboration with their peers 
allows for a totally supportive environment for academic 
achievement!  We are very proud of these students w ho 
personify many others who are preparing to take college 
classes next year and those who are currently enjoy ing 
collegial success!  The following students were invited up to 
receive a certificate and congratulations from the Board:  
Mohammad Abulaban, Amanda DiGiovanni, Parker Gilchrist, 
Erin Long, Nathan Murduff, and Carolyn Schaeper.    
Kris Piotrowski - Reading 
Recovery Teacher Leader 
recovery teacher leader certification.  The teacher leader 
candidate attends a registered university training center in the 
United States or a regional institute in Canada for an academic 
year of full-time professional development. Their complex role 
requires them to  
• become Reading Recovery teachers; 
• develop an academic understanding of the theoretical 
concepts upon which Reading Recovery is based and a 
flexibility to consider new concepts and practices; 
• test practice against theoretical concepts; 
• critically appraise Reading Recovery’s strengths and 
problem spots as well as competing explanations for its 
success; 
• observe and work through the experiences of a teacher 
trainee group across an academic year with the support of 
experienced teacher leaders in the field; 
• help teachers develop competency in individualized 
assessment-based instruction to improve student 
achievement; 
• become skilled at working with adult learners in order to 
effect significant change in teachers’ practice; 
• develop a thorough knowledge of the whole operation of 
Reading Recovery in an education system including 
organizing and administering the teacher training course 
and evaluating and reporting student outcomes; 
• become skilled at working with local administrators (e.g., 
site coordinator, principals); and 
• develop interactive system-level leadership skills. 
In order to accomplish these goals during the initial year of 
professional learning, teacher leader candidates teach four 
Reading Recovery students daily; participate in graduate-level 
Troy Board of Education Meeting 2 March 21, 2017 
10
Certification 
The Board recognized Kris Piotrowski for receiving her reading 
classes that include teaching sessions; engage in course work 
to explore theoretical concepts in reading, writing, language, 
literacy difficulties, and adult learning theory; participate in 
leadership seminars and practica that include field work at 
established sites; and prepare their home districts for Reading 
Recovery implementation.  Due to Kristin’s intense year-long 
training, Troy School District will be one of 15 sites in Michigan 
for Reading Recovery teachers.  We look forward to Kristin’s 
expertise impacting our Reading Recovery teachers and all 
students in the Troy School District.  This is exciting work that 
will take our Troy School District literacy work to world class 
status.  Thank you to Kristin for her hard work and dedication 
to her studies over the last year and years to come.   Kris was

WHEREAS,  the Troy School District Board of Education’s 
mission is to ensure learning for all members of the school 
community; and 
WHEREAS , the Troy School District Board of Education 
appreciates and values the real property owned by the District; 
and  
WHEREAS , the District desires to develop an Early Childhood 
Center to service our preschool programs; and  
WHEREAS , the Troy School District Board of Education desires 
to explore and consider the sale of certain surplus, vacant real 
property in order to offset long term debt for the development 
of an Early Childhood Center, and 
WHEREAS , the Troy School District Board of Education desires 
to authorize the School District’s Administration, its legal 
counsel, Clark Hill PLC, and its real estate consultant, Great 
Northern Consulting Group, to explore/market/analyze the sale 
of certain surplus, vacant real property, as outlined below, 
with the understanding that any Offer/Agreements etc. will be 
brought before the Board for approval, 
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED THAT:  
1.  The School District authorizes administration to further 
investigate the preservation of Section 1 property, with the 
exception of the frontage on Square Lake which will be 
split into its own parcel and marketed for sale .  The 
remainder of the land may be preserved in perpetuity 
either by the School District or through a conservation 
easement or other similar form of agreement. 
2.  The School District authorizes the marketing of Section 11 
property for purposes of sale.

Dr. Philippart asked Board members if there was any questions 
or discussion regarding this item.     
Mr. Hauff shared that he was in support of the Early Childhood 
Center.  He also indicated that he wanted additional time to 
review the property and consider the conversations and 
possible solutions to make an informed decision. 
Mr. Schmidt read a communication he received from a  
constituent related to the property.  He also clarified that he 
never described this as a simple issue.   
Mr. Gottlieb stated that he fully supports the Early Childhood 
Center provided there is a method or way to fund it and that it 
should be put out for open bid.  He also indicated that he 
would like to see further analysis done on the property and 
suggested postponing the decision for 1-2 weeks.   
Mrs. Hammond also supported the Early Childhood Center and 
suggested that community members visit the superintendent 
chats or make an appointment with other administrators to 
learn about the plans and the great things being done.   She 
did not agree with the land sale being tied with the Early 
Childhood Center in the resolution.

April 11, 2017 
Subject: Classroom A/V Displays Project (RFP #9839) Evaluation and Recommendation  
We received, opened and publicly read bids and proposals for Classroom Displays in the Secondary 
School Buildings at 1:30 P.M. March 10, 2017. The following is a summary of the evaluation and our 
recommendation. 
Three proposals were received: Digital Age Technologies (DAT), VSC and Tierney Brothers.  Several 
mandatory alternates were requested/required for alternate solutions. 
The initial results were tabulated as follows: 
Proposal Responses 
Bidder Displays  Base Bid   
Digital Age Technologies (DAT) Philips  $ 307,368.00  Middle School Only 
VSC SMART  $ 655,270.67  High and Middle Schools 
Tierney Brothers SMART & LG  $ 808,219.31  High and Middle Schools 
All proposals were evaluated for compliance with the RFP requirements, pricing, technical 
solution and acceptance of terms and conditions of the RFP. All Bidders provided compliant 
proposals and any exceptions or clarifications by the bidders were deemed acceptable.  DAT is not an 
authorized SMART reseller, therefore, DAT only included the middle school displays in their bid 
response.  Voluntary Alternates from Bidders were reviewed and discussed with the District. 
We requested further information and clarification from bidders and conducted a technical 
demonstration and bid interview with VSC on March 23, 2017.  
The Technology Committee and all Secondary Principals attended a demonstration by VSC.  
After the demonstration, the Technology Committee and all Secondary Principals remained to 
the discuss the potential solutions and options.  
The following discussions and clarifications were made with the Technology Committee and Secondary 
Principals: 
• The bid responses included non-interactive flat panel displays in the middle schools and 
interactive flat panel displays in the high schools based on RFP requirements. 
• Consideration of consistent interactive flat panel displays was discussed for the secondary level. 
• Deep discussions were had regarding individual department use of interactive displays and the 
benefit to deepen learning. 
122
• Discussions were also had with regards to the ability to use the interactive display as a center or 
remediation while other students are in work groups –  therefore increasing the ability for a 
collaborative space not tied to the front of the room. 
• The project includes an interactive flat panel display for all secondary classrooms that do not 
have a wall mounted, permanent display solution currently. 
• VSC, the recommended bidder, confirmed a single point of contact (Project manager) and daily 
online project updates will be provided to ensure project success. 
• VSC, the recommended bidder, confirmed that they will meet the project schedule outlined in 
the RFP (Completion October 30th). 
After final review, we proceeded with the low compliant bidder, VSC. The following modifications 
were made to the VSC base bid response for the recommended award total: 
Base bid $655,270.67  
Upgrade middle schools to interactive flat panels $120,269.84  
Deduct cabling ($ 56,796.66)  
Recommend award total $718,743.85  
Based on the above, we are recommending the award of the Classroom A/V Displays Project RFP #9839 
to VSC in the amount of the base bid amount of $718,743.85. 
Please contact me with any questions at 810-223-0528. 
Respectfully, 
John C. Foster, RCDD 
President 
Convergent Technology Partners

April 11, 2017 
Subject: Synchronized Clock System (RFP #9840) Evaluation and Recommendation  
We received, opened and publicly read bids and proposals for a complete, turn-key, district-wide 
Synchronized Clock System at 2:30 P.M. March 10, 2017. The following is a summary of the evaluation 
and our recommendation. 
Four proposals were received: Sound Engineering, Digital Age Technologies, I.COMM and Amcomm. No 
mandatory alternates were requested/required. 
The initial results were tabulated as follows: 
Proposal Responses 
Bidder System  Base Bid  
Sound Engineering Sapling  $ 391,473.00  
Digital Age Technologies (DAT) Carehawk  $ 403,787.00  
I.COMM Primex  $ 430,372.96  
Amcomm Primex  $ 492,000.00  
All proposals were evaluated for compliance with the RFP requirements, pricing, technical 
solution and acceptance of terms and conditions of the RFP. All Bidders provided compliant 
proposals and none of the bidders took any exceptions (See notes on transportation building below). 
We requested further information and clarification from bidders on March 16, 2017. As all systems met 
the RFP requirements including additional clarification requested, we proceeded with the low compliant 
bidder, Sound Engineering. 
The following clarifications were made: 
• The proposed digital clock is Sapling Model is SBL31S4041R, the unit is a 4-digit model 
• Countdown functionality is available and will be provided and programmed at no additional cost 
to the district for all secondary buildings as requested by the principals 
• Digital clocks will show hours and minutes in normal mode, switch to minutes and seconds 
during count-down mode, then revert to normal mode after the countdown is complete 
• The clock system will synchronize with the district time server on an hourly basis 
• The clock system will integrate with the existing PA system and correct the PA system time once 
a day at 2:00 A.M. PA system integration is at no cost to the district 
• Sound Engineering, the recommended bidder, confirmed a single point of contact (Project 
manager) and weekly online written status reports will be provided to ensure project success 
• Sound Engineering, the recommended bidder, confirmed that they will meet the project 
schedule outlined in the RFP (Completion August 4th) and that installation will be by building. 
126
Once complete, the building will be moved to the new system prior to commencing 
implementation in the next building 
Note: Drawings for the transportation building were not available during the RFP process and therefore 
pricing was not included in any proposal. We are requesting an allowance of $15,000.00 to cover this 
building and any other clocks not discovered during our walk through of the District buildings. 
We recommend the award of the Synchronized Clock System RFP #9840 to Sound Engineering in the 
amount of base bid amount of $391,473.00 with an additional allowance of $15,000.00 for a total of 
$406,473.00. 
Please contact me with any questions at 810-223-0528. 
Respectfully, 
John C. Foster, RCDD 
President 
Convergent Technology Partners
